 ▎ Tackle your toughest work with Opus 5. Switch anytime with /model.

● That's almost certainly your machine's firewall blocking inbound connections
  from other devices — the server is correctly bound to all interfaces (*:5173),
  so it's not a binding problem. Checking the firewall needs your sudo
  password, which I can't provide. Run this yourself (type ! followed by the
  command so it runs in this session):

  ! sudo ufw status

  If it says "Status: active" and there's no rule for 5173, that's the cause. To
  fix it:

  ! sudo ufw allow 5173/tcp comment 'vite dev server'

  A couple of other things worth checking if that doesn't fix it:
  1. Confirm your phone is actually on the same Wi-Fi network as this PC (not a
  guest network or mobile data)
  2. Some routers have "client/AP isolation" enabled (common on guest networks)
  that blocks devices on the same Wi-Fi from reaching each other even with no
  firewall involved — if ufw isn't the issue, that's the next suspect, and
  there's nothing I can fix locally for that, it'd need a router setting change.

❯ ❯ So one extra requirement for the pizzas is that clients can pick a pizza of 
  2, 3 or 4 flavors and split it as wanted. For example a client orders a       
  pizza xl with max 4 flavors and orders 50% 25% 25% instead of 25% 25 25 25 or 
  in a 3 flavor pizza 33 33 33 or 50 25 25. So im thinking the way we can       
  tackle this req is by augmenting the order_item_flavors table to add porcion  
  represented as a number of percentage. Porcion need now to be part of the     
  order creation object flavor is now an array of object with two values flavor 
  and porcion. For the frontend side we need a way to represent this when       
  choosing the flavors, i was thinking of a svg of a pizza that the waitress    
  can paint but this wouldnt allow 33 33 33 or 50 25 25. some combinations are  
  not possible so please recommend a way to represent this fe side.

● Agent "Find pizza flavor/order schema code" finished · 1m 1s

  Searched for 1 pattern, read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● Good news: the schema already has exactly what this needs. pizza_sizes.slices
  (6/8/10/12/14 depending on size) exists but isn't used for portions today —
  flavors are just a flat order_item_flavors(order_item_id, flavor_id) join with
  no weight, and pricing sums every flavor's full extra_cost regardless of
  split (orderService.ts resolvePizzaItem).

  Backend — your instinct is right, with two refinements:

  1. Add portion INTEGER NOT NULL (percent, 0–100) to order_item_flavors.
  Validate server-side in resolvePizzaItem that portions for an item sum to
  exactly 100 — SQLite can't enforce a cross-row sum in a CHECK constraint, so
  this has to be app-level, same place flavors.length > max_flavors is already
  checked.
  2. PizzaItemRequest.flavors changes from string[] to { flavorId: string; 
  portion: number }[] (mirrored in PizzaRef and the frontend types/api.ts copy).
  Pricing becomes extraCost = flavors.reduce((sum, f) => sum + f.extra_cost * 
  (f.portion / 100), 0).

  One thing worth deciding before I touch code: today a single "special" flavor
  upgrades the entire pizza to special pricing regardless of quantity. With
  portions, does a special flavor at 10% still upgrade the whole pie, or should
  the upsell scale with portion too? I'd lean toward keeping "any special flavor
  present → special price" (simplest, matches current behavior, and is
  defensible since the kitchen still has to handle a special topping) — but flag
  it since it's a real behavior choice.

  Frontend — don't do freehand painting, use a discrete pattern picker instead.
  Freehand (paint/drag on an SVG) can produce any fraction, which is exactly the
  problem you flagged — you'd need to snap/validate afterward anyway, and it's
  fiddly on a POS touchscreen where speed matters more than precision. Instead:

  - Keep today's flavor-toggle step unchanged (tap up to max_flavors flavors).
  - Once N flavors are selected, if N is 3 or 4, show 2–3 tappable pattern chips
  instead of freeform input — e.g. for 3 flavors: "Partes iguales (33/33/33)"
  vs "Mitad y mitad (50/25/25)"; for 4: "Partes iguales (25/25/25/25)" (add more
  patterns later only if you actually need them). N=1 and N=2 need no picker at
  all (100%, and 50/50 are the only sensible options).
  - If they pick an asymmetric pattern (50/25/25), one more tap lets them mark
  which selected flavor gets the 50% slice (default: first one tapped).
  - Render a read-only pizza-wedge preview (CSS conic-gradient is enough, no
  need for real SVG geometry) so they can visually confirm before adding to cart
  — this is just UI feedback, not data; the DB doesn't need wedge position,
  only flavor+portion pairs.

  This is 2–3 taps total, always produces a valid, sum-to-100 split, and needs
  no drag/gesture handling. One rounding note: 33/33/33 doesn't divide 100
  evenly — I'd store it as 34/33/33 (remainder to the first flavor) since
  integer percent is simpler to reason about than fractions everywhere else in
  the codebase.

  Want me to go ahead and implement this (schema migration + orderService.ts +
  types on both sides + the FE pattern-picker component)?

         +: string): void {                                              
      51 +  const columns = db.prepare(`PRAGMA table_info(${table})`).all
         +() as { name: string }[];                                      
      52 +  if (!columns.some((c) => c.name === column)) {               
      53 +    db.exec(`ALTER TABLE ${table} ADD COLUMN ${column} ${defini

      204 +/** Percent -> the simplified fraction it represents, e.g. 25 
          +-> "1/4". Empty for a whole (100%) flavor. */                 
      205 +function formatPortionFraction(portion: number): string {     
      206 +  if (portion >= 100) return '';                              
      207 +  // 100/3 isn't an integer, so equal thirds are stored as 34/
          +33/33 - still just "1/3" to a reader.                         
      208 +  if (portion === 33 || portion === 34) return '1/3';         
      209 +  const divisor = gcd(portion, 100);                          
      210 +  return `${portion / divisor}/${100 / divisor}`;             
      211 +}                                                             
      212 +                                                              
      213 +function gcd(a: number, b: number): number {                  
      214 +  return b === 0 ? a : gcd(b, a % b);                         
      215 +}                                                             
      216 +

          +zzaFlavorName.get(f.flavor)?.name ?? f.flavor);

       63    return 1;

       65  
       66 +/**                                                           
       67 + * How a pizza can be split by number of flavors. Only 3-flavo
          +r pizzas have a                                               
       68 + * real choice: equal thirds, or one flavor at half with the o
          +ther two at a                                                 
       69 + * quarter each. 1, 2, and 4 flavors only ever split evenly.  
       70 + */                                                           
       71 +export type FlavorSplitPattern = 'equal' | 'half';            
       72 +                                                              
       73 +export function splitPatternsFor(flavorCount: number): FlavorS
          +plitPattern[] {                                               
       74 +  return flavorCount === 3 ? ['equal', 'half'] : ['equal'];   
       75 +}                                                             
       76 +                                                              
       77 +/**                                                           
       78 + * Turns selected flavor ids + a chosen pattern into { flavor,
          + portion } pairs                                              
       79 + * summing to exactly 100. 'equal' thirds can't divide evenly 
          +(100/3), so                                                   
       80 + * that remainder goes to the first selected flavor (34/33/33)
          +.                                                             
       81 + */                                                           
       82 +export function computeFlavorPortions(                        
       83 +  selectedFlavors: string[],                                  
       84 +  pattern: FlavorSplitPattern,                                
       85 +  halfFlavorId?: string,                                      
       86 +): { flavor: string; portion: number }[] {                    
       87 +  const n = selectedFlavors.length;                           
       88 +  if (n === 0) return [];                                     
       89 +  if (pattern === 'half' && n === 3) {                        
       90 +    const big = halfFlavorId ?? selectedFlavors[0];           
       91 +    return selectedFlavors.map((f) => ({ flavor: f, portion: f
          + === big ? 50 : 25 }));                                       
       92 +  }                                                           
       93 +  const base = Math.floor(100 / n);                           
       94 +  const remainder = 100 - base * n;                           
       95 +  return selectedFlavors.map((f, i) => ({ flavor: f, portion: 
          +i === 0 ? base + remainder : base }));                        
       96 +}                                                             
       97 +                                                              
       98 +/** Percent -> the simplified fraction it represents, e.g. 25 
          +-> "1/4". Empty for a whole (100%) flavor. */                 
       99 +export function formatPortionFraction(portion: number): string
          + {                                                            
      100 +  if (portion >= 100) return '';                              
      101 +  // 100/3 isn't an integer, so equal thirds are stored as 34/
          +33/33 - still just "1/3" to a reader.                         
      102 +  if (portion === 33 || portion === 34) return '1/3';         
      103 +  const divisor = gcd(portion, 100);                          
      104 +  return `${portion / divisor}/${100 / divisor}`;             
      105 +}                                                             
      106 +                                                              
      107 +function gcd(a: number, b: number): number {                  
      108 +  return b === 0 ? a : gcd(b, a % b);                         
      109 +}                                                             
      110 +                                                              
      111  export function productUnitPrice(product: Product, sizeId?: st
           ring): number {
      112    if (product.sizes && product.sizes.length > 0) {
      113      return product.sizes.find((s) => s.id === sizeId)?.price ?
           ? 0;

● Done. Summary of the multi-flavor split-percentage feature, fully implemented
  and tested end-to-end:

  Backend
  - order_item_flavors gets a portion INTEGER column (percent, 1–100);
  schema.sql for fresh DBs, plus a non-destructive ALTER TABLE in migrate.ts for
  the existing DB (ran it — your data was untouched, verified before/after).
  - PizzaItemRequest.flavors / PizzaRef.flavors are now { flavor, portion }[]
  instead of string[], mirrored in both backend and frontend types.
  - resolvePizzaItem validates portions sum to exactly 100, and extra cost now
  scales by portion (extra_cost * portion/100) — verified empirically: bumping a
  flavor's extra_cost and giving it 25% share added exactly a quarter of that
  cost to the total, then reverted the test data.
  - Group upsell is untouched by design, per your call: any special flavor
  present pulls the whole pizza to special pricing regardless of portion —
  confirmed a 25%-portion special flavor still priced the pie as "special".
  - Comanda (kitchen ticket) now prints a fraction next to each flavor, e.g.
  Margarita (1/2), BBQ (1/4), Hawaiana (1/4) — confirmed against a real printed
  ticket. Thirds (33/34 split, since 100 isn't divisible by 3) both simplify to
  "1/3" on the ticket.

  Frontend (menu/pizzas/$size.tsx)
  - Same flavor-toggle interaction as before, but selected tiles now show a %
  badge.
  - When exactly 3 flavors are selected, two pattern chips appear: "Partes
  iguales (33/33/33)" and "Mitad y mitad (50/25/25)" — picking the latter lets
  you tap which flavor gets the half. 1, 2, and 4 flavors always split evenly
  (100/50-50/25-25-25-25), no picker needed.
  - Cart label and the order-overview panel both show flavor names with their
  fraction.

  Also updated the two dev WS test scripts (simulate-orders.ts,
  test-order-client.ts) and the ER diagram to the new shape so they don't break.
  Both server and frontend typecheck clean.

  One thing to flag: your own backend dev process (the tsx watch one you started
  earlier) is still running but stopped listening on any port partway through —
  port 3001 is dead even though the process is alive. I tested everything
  against an isolated instance instead of touching your terminal, but you'll
  want to check that terminal and restart it (Ctrl+C then npm run dev again) to
  pick up these changes live.

● Added a momentary full-screen confirmation modal, mounted globally so it
  survives navigation (e.g. the "closed" one fires right as the page redirects
  to /tables):

  - New order sent → an up-arrow draws into a ring, "¡Orden enviada!" / "La
  cocina ya la tiene en cola."
  - Order closed/paid → a checkmark draws into a ring, "¡Orden cobrada!" / "La
  cuenta quedó cerrada."

  Both use a hand-built animated SVG (stroke-dasharray draw-in, no external
  libs), sit over a backdrop-blur-sm dark scrim, and auto-dismiss after ~2.2s —
  no dismiss button needed since it's meant to be momentary.

● Fixed and verified. Summary:

  Root cause: PaymentModal's completeOrder() call discarded the API response —
  every other order mutation in OrderOverview.tsx (tip, delivery fee, new items,
  order creation) calls upsertActiveOrder() afterward to sync the store, but
  completing an order never did, so the just-closed order stayed in activeOrders
  and kept showing in "Órdenes activas" until a manual refresh.

  Fix: PaymentModal now returns the completed order from completeOrder(), and
  OrderOverview.handlePaymentSuccess passes it to upsertActiveOrder() — which
  already drops any order with status === 'COMPLETED' from the list. Verified
  live: closed a takeaway order and it disappeared from the dropdown
  immediately, no reload needed.

  One side note: while testing I hit a one-off duplicate-entry glitch in the
  dropdown that I couldn't reproduce across three clean re-runs, and the server
  API only ever returned the correct single order — looks like a fluke from
  dev-server churn during testing rather than a real bug, but flagging it in
  case you ever see a duplicate row live.

✻ Baked for 7m 24s

※ recap: Building the Dinapoli POS frontend/backend with iterative UI fixes; just finished making "Órdenes activas" drop closed orders correctly. Next: continue addressing your remaining feedback, or let me know what's next. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ so basically you mark that order as completed in the store and then for       
  persistency hit the api right?                                                

● Not quite — it's the other way around. The API call happens first, and the
  store update is driven by its response:

  // PaymentModal.tsx
  const completedOrder = await completeOrder(order.id, splits...); // 1. hits 
  POST /orders/:id/complete, awaits the server
  reset();
  onSuccess(completedOrder);                                       // 2. hands 
  the server's response up

  // OrderOverview.tsx
  const handlePaymentSuccess = (completedOrder: Order) => {
    upsertActiveOrder(completedOrder); // 3. store syncs from what the server 
  actually returned
    ...
  };

  So there's no optimistic "mark it completed in the store, then persist" step —
  the store is only ever updated after the server confirms completion, using
  the exact order object the server sent back (status COMPLETED, completedAt
  set, etc.). upsertActiveOrder then drops it from activeOrders because its
  status is no longer active. This avoids the store ever showing "completed"
  before the server actually agrees it's completed — no rollback logic needed if
  the request fails, since the store just never got touched in that case.

      409 +function CartRow({ group, onRemoveOne, onRemoveAll }: { group:
          + GroupedCartItem; onRemoveOne: () => void; onRemoveAll: () => 
          +void }) {                                                     
      410 +  const timerRef = useRef<ReturnType<typeof setTimeout> | null
          +>(null);                                                      
      411 +  const longPressFiredRef = useRef(false);                    
      412 +                                                              
      413 +  const startPress = () => {                                  
      414 +    longPressFiredRef.current = false;                        
      415 +    timerRef.current = setTimeout(() => {                     
      416 +      longPressFiredRef.current = true;                       
      417 +      onRemoveAll();                                          
      418 +    }, LONG_PRESS_MS);                                        
      419 +  };                                                          
      420 +  const cancelPress = () => {                                 
      421 +    if (timerRef.current) clearTimeout(timerRef.current);     
      422 +  };

          +llow the click                                                
      425 +    // that follows mouseup/touchend so it doesn't also remove
          + one more.                                                    
      426 +    if (longPressFiredRef.current) {                          
      427 +      longPressFiredRef.current = false;                      
      428 +      return;                                                 
      429 +    }                                                         
      430 +    onRemoveOne();                                            
      431 +  };                                                          
      432 +                                                              
      433 +  return (

✻ Worked for 8m 44s

❯ just spotted a DB bug, after picking mixed payment in the db i see null       
  instead of the selected payment methods.                                      

● That's actually intentional, not a bug — let me show you why and check whether
  the real data is there.

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

● That's expected, not a bug — for order 28, the two payments are correctly
  recorded:

  order_payments: { order_id: 28, method: 'cash', amount: 20000 }
                   { order_id: 28, method: 'card', amount: 21000 }

  orders.payment_method only holds a single method, so when settlement uses more
  than one (a mixed payment), it's deliberately set back to NULL — a single
  column literally can't represent "cash + card". The real breakdown always
  lives in order_payments (one row per method used); the comment right above
  that column in schema.sql:96-100 documents this. Every report in the app
  (End-of-Day closing, cash sales, etc.) already reads from order_payments, not
  orders.payment_method, for exactly this reason.

  If browsing the raw orders table and seeing NULL there is confusing when
  you're just poking around the DB, I could add a 'mixed' sentinel value instead
  of NULL — but that'd mean loosening the CHECK (payment_method IN 
  ('cash','card','transfer')) constraint to also allow 'mixed', and it wouldn't
  change how anything actually uses the field. Want that, or is knowing where
  the real data lives enough?

      58 +/** Drops a column from a table that predates its removal. No-o
         +p if already gone. */                                          
      59 +function dropColumnIfExists(table: string, column: string): voi
         +d {                                                            
      60 +  const columns = db.prepare(`PRAGMA table_info(${table})`).all
         +() as { name: string }[];                                      
      61 +  if (columns.some((c) => c.name === column)) {                
      62 +    db.exec(`ALTER TABLE ${table} DROP COLUMN ${column}`);     
      63 +  }                                                            
      64 +}

● Done. Summary:

  orders.payment_method fully removed — schema, migration (dropped live from
  your dev DB, all 19 orders preserved), backend types/service logic, frontend
  types, and PaymentModal's default. Verified against a clean server instance:
  mixed payments now record correctly and only in order_payments (tested cash
  $10,000 + card $12,000 → both rows correct, no more phantom NULL), and the API
  response no longer has a paymentMethod field at all. One intentional behavior
  change: /complete now always requires an explicit payments array — the old
  "complete with no body, fall back to a pre-declared method" shortcut is gone,
  since nothing in your frontend ever used it (confirmed: order creation over WS
  never sent paymentMethod, and PaymentModal always sends payments explicitly).
  Tested that path too — it now 400s with a clear message instead of silently
  working.

  Heads up: your own terminal's dev server (tsx watch) hadn't picked up the
  change when I tested through the real UI — it threw table orders has no column
  named payment_method, meaning it's running stale code against the
  now-migrated schema. You'll want to restart it (Ctrl+C, npm run dev again).

  On tip/deliveryFee — these aren't the same situation, don't remove them:
  - orders.tip is the single live value, editable any time via PUT 
  /orders/:id/tip — including long before an order is ever paid (the
  10%/20%/Otra buttons work on an active, unpaid order).
  order_payments.tip_amount only exists after completion and is a per-method
  breakdown that must sum to orders.tip — it can't stand in for it, since
  there's nothing to break down until payment happens.
  - orders.delivery_fee has no representation at all in order_payments — no
  column for it, nowhere it's decomposed. End-of-day reporting reads it straight
  off orders.

  payment_method was removable because it was genuinely a mirror/duplicate of
  settled data. Tip and delivery fee aren't duplicates of anything — removing
  either would break live editing before checkout and delete data that exists
  nowhere else.

  Frontend request audit (the second part of your ask): checked every
  POST/PUT/WS call site — order creation (WS), tip PUT, delivery-fee PUT,
  add-items POST, and the complete POST. All of them already send the right
  shape; the PaymentModal tip fix from earlier already made sure the tip PUT
  fires before /complete so the amounts reconcile server-side. Nothing else
  needed changing there.

         +ns get dropped,

      61 +function backfillOrderSettlement(): void {                     
      62 +  const columns = db.prepare(`PRAGMA table_info(orders)`).all()
         + as { name: string }[];                                        
      63 +  const hasTip = columns.some((c) => c.name === 'tip');        
      64 +  const hasDeliveryFee = columns.some((c) => c.name === 'delive
         +ry_fee');                                                      
      65 +  if (!hasTip && !hasDeliveryFee) return;                      
      66 +  db.exec(`                                                    
      67 +    INSERT INTO order_settlement (order_id, tip, delivery_fee) 
      68 +    SELECT id, ${hasTip ? 'tip' : '0'}, ${hasDeliveryFee ? 'del
         +ivery_fee' : '0'} FROM orders                                  
      69 +    WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT order_id FROM order_settlement)    
      70 +  `);                                                          
      71 +}                                                              
      72 +                                                               
      73  /** Adds a column to a table that predates it, without touching
           existing rows. No-op if already present. */

          () as { name: string }[];

● All done. Summary of this whole arc:

  Architecture change: orders.tip/orders.delivery_fee are gone — moved to a new
  order_settlement table (one row per order, live/editable anytime pre- or
  post-checkout via PUT /orders/:id/{tip,delivery-fee,discount}). order_payments
  gained delivery_fee and discount columns alongside the existing tip_amount,
  so the full per-method breakdown lives in one normalized place. Migration ran
  clean on your dev DB, no data loss beyond the earlier reset that already
  happened.

  Discount feature (new, backend + frontend):
  - order_settlement.discount (live, pre-checkout) and order_payments.discount
  (per-method breakdown at checkout), mirroring tip exactly.
  - Critical property, verified live: order_payments.amount always stores the
  gross pre-discount charge — never reduced. Tested a $71,000 gross charge with
  a $5,000 discount: amount: 71000, discount: 5000 both stored, net collected
  (amount - discount = 66000) only ever derived, never persisted. Also tested a
  discount split across two payment methods (mixed payment) — sums reconcile
  correctly.
  - End-of-Day sales and the printed customer bill both now subtract discount
  (it's revenue you didn't collect, same treatment logic as tip but for a
  different reason).
  - Order Overview panel: new "Descuento" row, positioned above "Propina" as
  asked.
  - Payment Modal: new "Descuento" column. The Monto a cobrar field represents
  net cash collected (intuitive for the cashier, consistent with how
  tip/delivery fee already auto-adjust it); the gross figure sent to the API is
  reconstructed as Monto + Descuento at submit time — so the UI stays simple
  while the DB still gets "20 should still be recorded."

  Order History price fix: now shows total + tip + deliveryFee - discount,
  guarded against any of the three being missing.

  One more thing: your live dev server needs another restart to pick up this
  schema change (same as before) — Ctrl+C then npm run dev.

✻ Crunched for 3m 9s

❯ why is there an order settlement table now i dont get it it has repeated data 
  like tip and delivery fee which is already present in order_payments table    
  wtf is going on                                                               

● Because order_payments rows don't exist yet for most of an order's life — they
  only get created once, at checkout, one row per payment method. Before that
  (while the order is PENDING/ACTIVE, being built, sitting in the kitchen),
  there are zero order_payments rows at all.

  order_settlement is what lets staff set the tip/delivery fee/discount before a
  payment method is even chosen — the 10%/20%/Otra tip buttons, the delivery
  fee field, the discount field in the Order Overview panel all work on an order
  that hasn't been paid yet, sometimes way before checkout. There's nowhere in
  order_payments to write that, since every row there requires a method
  (cash/card/transfer), which doesn't exist yet.

  So it's not duplicated data, it's the same value at two different points in
  the order's life:
  - order_settlement.tip — the live, still-changeable value, one row per order,
  editable any time via PUT /orders/:id/tip.
  - order_payments.tip_amount (summed across however many payment rows) — the
  frozen, final breakdown by payment method, written once at completion.

  They'll only look "redundant" once an order is already COMPLETED, because by
  then they're required to match exactly (that's enforced — sum(tip_amount) === 
  order_settlement.tip, checked in resolvePayments). Before completion, only
  order_settlement has anything in it.

  This was actually the exact tradeoff I asked you about a few messages back — I
  offered two options: (a) only allow entering tip/discount at checkout time
  and skip a new table entirely, or (b) keep pre-checkout editing and add
  order_settlement for it. You picked (b) explicitly ("make it editable
  pre-checkout... modify the API to make it work with the new table"), which is
  why it exists.

  If you'd rather simplify to option (a) now — drop order_settlement, remove the
  pre-checkout tip/delivery-fee/discount buttons from Order Overview, and only
  let staff enter them in the Payment Modal at checkout — I can do that instead.
  Want me to switch to that?

● Everything working correctly. Cleaning up all test artifacts:

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 4 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

● Both clean. Summary:

  Fully reverted the order_settlement approach. Tip, delivery fee, and discount
  are now only ever declared once, as part of payments[] in POST 
  /orders/:id/complete — the same design as payment_method before it.
  Order.tip/deliveryFee/discount are derived by summing order_payments
  (COALESCE(SUM(...), 0)), which is naturally 0 for anything not yet completed
  and the real total once it is — no special-casing needed.

  Pre-checkout editing now lives entirely in the Zustand store
  (pendingTip/pendingDeliveryFee/pendingDiscount in useOrderStore), purely
  client-side, no network calls while the order is being built. PaymentModal
  reads those as its starting values and sends everything in one shot at
  completion.

  Found and fixed one real bug during verification: submitting a new order
  called openExistingOrder() to transition into "existing" mode, which — by
  design — resets the pending drafts for switching to a different order, but

   Two separate pieces, both in server/src/db/:

      1. The connection (node ↔ db)

      This isn't a network connection — better-sqlite3 is a native binding that opens the .db file directly in the same process, synchronously. There's a single module (something like server/src/db/index.ts) that does roughly:

      const db = new Database(pathToDbFile);
      db.pragma('journal_mode = WAL');
      export default db;

      That module creates one shared Database instance and exports it as a default export. Every service in the app (orderService, promoSettingsService, etc.) imports that same instance directly, e.g. import db from '../db/index.js', and calls db.prepare(...) / db.exec(...) on it. There's no pooling or per-request connecting — it's one persistent handle to the file for the life of the server process.

    2. Where tables get created, and how "already exists" is checked

    - server/src/db/schema.sql holds the full set of CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS ... statements for every table in the app.
    - server/src/db/migrate.ts is the script that actually applies it: it runs db.exec(schema) (loading schema.sql's full text and executing it as one batch). Because every statement is IF NOT EXISTS, this is safe to run against a database that already has some or all of those tables — SQLite itself skips creating anything that's already there. That's the actual "check if it exists" — it's baked into the SQL syntax, not a manual JS if branch.
    - For changes that aren't "create a new table" (e.g. adding a column to a table that predates that column), migrate.ts has helper functions — ensureColumn, renameColumnIfExists, dropColumnIfExists — that do the manual check themselves, by running PRAGMA table_info(table) and inspecting the returned column list before deciding whether to ALTER TABLE. That's needed because SQLite's ALTER TABLE ADD COLUMN has no IF NOT EXISTS form the way CREATE TABLE does.
      - After db.exec(schema) and those column-level helpers, migrate.ts also calls a handful of seedDefault*() functions (for things like promo_settings, cash_register_settings) that use INSERT OR IGNORE, so default rows get inserted once and never overwritten on later runs.

      So the full sequence on every migrate.ts run is: execute schema.sql (creates anything missing, no-ops on anything present) → run the column-level migration helpers (each self-checks via PRAGMA table_info) → run the seed functions (each self-checks via INSERT OR IGNORE).
